#2751. Crypto talent will return ‘when AI implodes,’ Arthur Hayes says

Startup talent that has concentrated in artificial intelligence will return to the crypto industry "when AI implodes," according to Arthur Hayes, chief investment officer at Maelstrom Fund.

"A lot of talent has gone into the AI space, so I definitely think there'll be a resurgence in good founder talent coming back to crypto," Hayes said in an online interview with CNBC on Tuesday.

He added that some founders may also return once they "recognize" that their products could eventually be "usurped by Anthropic or OpenAI."

The scale and capabilities of the largest frontier AI labs have made it difficult to "build a real AI business," Hayes said, arguing that "most" founders simply "raise a bunch of money and then light it on fire."

He expects the AI trade to face "serious headwinds" in the second half of 2027, when leading labs begin confronting sizable bills tied to compute leases and other non-cancelable commitments.

Anthropic expects about $451.6 billion in compute and infrastructure obligations through the end of 2030, with roughly $70.7 billion due by the end of September 2027, according to its prospectus.

"Do they have the money? We're going to find out," Hayes said.

Broader enthusiasm around the AI trade has also cooled amid questions about future infrastructure spending. Even exuberance from Jensen Huang's return as Nvidia CEO on Monday proved short-lived, with the shares falling 5.9% on Tuesday and Anthropic dropping below its IPO price four days after its debut.

The co-founder of now-shuttered crypto exchange BitMEX also thinks bitcoin could test $50,000 if the Nasdaq continues to fall. The cryptocurrency has shown little sign of recovery after dropping as low as $55,142.16 on Saturday — its lowest level since July.

Bitcoin hit an all-time high of $126,279.63 in October last year and traded as high as $97,915.90 in January this year.

It has now fallen for seven straight weeks — only the third such streak in its history — with crypto exchange Bitfinex attributing the weakness to equity-market sentiment rather than interest-rate expectations.

Hayes said he would like to see bitcoin rebound from around $55,000 even as the Nasdaq continues to fall. That divergence, he said, would suggest markets were shifting from a broad sell-off toward pricing in a "reflation" scenario, with the Federal Reserve eventually providing more liquidity.

Neither bitcoin nor gold has yet decoupled from the Nasdaq, with Hayes describing the current market moves as a "correlation-led liquidation."

"Stuff goes down, you sell crypto first because it's easy, and then you sell everything else," he said.

Hayes said a bitcoin rally could push up funding rates and futures-basis returns, increasing the income Ethena earns from its hedged crypto positions. That could make USDe, Ethena's synthetic dollar, more attractive and expand its circulation, he said, adding that he expects the ENA token to rise materially.

"Those are the two things that I'm keeping an eye on, and when I'm ready to pull the trigger on buying risk again, those will be the things we buy a lot of," Hayes said.

#2747. Nick Saban Reveals Where Alabama Stands in National Championship Race

TUSCALOOSA, Ala. — Nick Saban remains one of the most influential voices in college football, even after stepping away from the Alabama sideline.

However, the legendary Crimson Tide coach recently offered an assessment that could give Alabama fans something to debate around the Thanksgiving table.

With the Iron Bowl approaching and the College Football Playoff race intensifying, Saban identified three programs he believes possess the ingredients to win a national championship.

During a Friday, Nov. 20, appearance on ESPN's "The Pat McAfee Show," Saban discussed the characteristics that separate legitimate national championship contenders from the rest of the field.

The seven-time national champion identified Georgia, Utah and Notre Dame as three particularly complete teams.

His reasoning centered on quarterback play, defensive performance and the ability to establish a productive running game.

Saban believes those three programs have demonstrated the balance necessary to navigate the postseason.

However, the former Alabama coach stopped short of placing his old program alongside them.

The omission stands out as Alabama prepares for one of its most important games of the season.

Although Alabama did not make Saban's three-team shortlist, the former coach did not dismiss the Crimson Tide's championship chances.

Instead, he acknowledged that several additional programs could realistically compete for the national title.

"This is no disrespect to Alabama," Saban said during the ESPN appearance.

He subsequently included the Crimson Tide among a larger group of approximately seven or eight teams capable of winning the championship.

Nevertheless, Saban suggested that Alabama and other contenders have weaknesses that separate them from his preferred three.

The distinction offers an interesting perspective from the coach who built Alabama into college football's dominant program.

Saban won six national championships during his 17 seasons in Tuscaloosa before retiring following the 2023 season.

Now, his position as an ESPN analyst allows him to evaluate the Crimson Tide alongside the rest of the country.

Saban's assessment arrives as Alabama prepares to host Auburn on Saturday at Bryant-Denny Stadium.

The Crimson Tide enter the rivalry matchup with a 9-2 record and remain firmly involved in the College Football Playoff conversation.

Meanwhile, Auburn brings a 7-4 record into Tuscaloosa under first-year coach Alex Golesh.

Alabama has won six consecutive Iron Bowls, including victories in each of Kalen DeBoer's first two seasons.

However, the Tigers have an opportunity to complicate Alabama's postseason outlook with an upset.

The Crimson Tide must also demonstrate that their offense can consistently produce against quality competition.

That concern connects directly to Saban's assessment of the championship race.

Although Alabama possesses the talent to compete with the nation's best teams, questions remain about its overall consistency.

Saturday provides another opportunity for DeBoer's team to address those concerns.

The comments also arrive during a season in which Saban's legacy has received renewed attention.

ESPN released its six-part documentary series, "Saban," in October, providing an extensive look at his coaching career and personal life.

The series features interviews with former players, coaches and family members, including his wife, Terry Saban.

Additionally, it explores the demanding standards that helped Saban build championship programs at LSU and Alabama.

His comments about the current playoff race reflect many of those same principles.

Rather than focusing exclusively on records or rankings, Saban continues to emphasize complete football teams.

That philosophy helped define his coaching career and remains central to his analysis.

Alabama still has opportunities to strengthen its postseason position, beginning with Saturday's Iron Bowl.

A convincing victory over Auburn would provide another positive result before the final College Football Playoff selections.

However, the Crimson Tide must eventually demonstrate the consistency Saban identified in Georgia, Utah and Notre Dame.

For Alabama fans, the former coach's assessment presents an interesting question during Thanksgiving week.

Does the Crimson Tide belong among the nation's most complete championship contenders, or does Saban see weaknesses that could become costly in December?

The answer will ultimately come on the field.

Alabama and Auburn kick off Saturday at 7:30 p.m. ET on ABC.

#2746. Fed’s preferred inflation gauge accelerated to 3.8% in October, raising the 2027 stakes for the central bank

Inflation moved further away from the Federal Reserve's target in October, reinforcing expectations that another interest rate hike is in the cards for December and raising questions about how much more work the central bank will have to do in 2027.

The personal consumption expenditures price index, the Fed's preferred inflation gauge, showed a seasonally adjusted 3.8% annual inflation rate, in line with the Dow Jones consensus estimate and nearly twice the central bank's goal. The reading was 0.1 percentage point higher than September.

Excluding food and energy, core PCE was 3.3%, the Commerce Department reported Wednesday. That was 0.1 percentage point above the forecast.

While the Fed officially follows the headline figure, policymakers consider core to be a more reliable longer-term gauge of inflation trends.

A separate report also showed much stronger economic growth for the third quarter, with gross domestic product rising at a 4.3% annualized rate, according to a revised Commerce Department estimate. The strong top-line gain for the economy adds further fuel to the notion that Fed monetary policy is not restricting economic activity.

Consumer spending rose 0.3% for the month, which matched the pace of price increases but was 0.1 percentage point lower than forecast. Income, on the other hand, surged 0.7%, much more than the 0.4% estimate. The personal saving rate rose to 4.1%.

Stock market futures added to losses following the release, while Treasury yields also were sharply lower. Even with the higher-than-expected core inflation figure, expectations eased a bit for Fed rate hikes in 2027. Traders continued to price in two more increases but lowered odds for a third hike to about 1-in-3, compared to 50-50 on Tuesday, according to the CME Group's FedWatch.

"Inflation is too hot and it will not be brought under control as long as economic demand is also too hot," said Chris Rupkey, chief economist at Fwdbonds.

The report comes with the market fully pricing in a quarter percentage point interest rate hike when the Fed meets Dec. 8-9. Less certain is how much more rates will need to increase to get inflation back down to the Fed's 2% target. Inflation has been running above target for some 5½ years.

Inflation had been drifting lower but spiked again following the late-February attacks on Iran led by the U.S. and Israel. Tariffs also have continued to exert pressure, as has strong demand associated with the artificial intelligence buildout. The White House on Wednesday announced a fresh round of tariffs on 14 trading partners including China, the European Union and Japan.

Policymakers generally consider factors such as tariffs and supply shocks to be temporary, but some fear the impact has begun spreading through the economy and could become embedded.

"America's inflation problem is bigger than an energy shock," said Heather Long, chief economist at Navy Federal Credit Union.

The large upward increase in GDP came from revisions to investment as well as consumer and government spending.

Consumer spending, which accounts for about two-thirds of all economic activity, rose 4.2% in the third quarter. A metric known as real final sales to private domestic purchasers, which Fed officials regard as a key indicator of underlying demand, jumped 5.8%, reflecting an upward revision of 0.9 percentage point.

In other economic news Wednesday, the Labor Department reported that initial jobless claims slipped to 189,000 for the week ending Nov. 21, down 13,000 from the prior week and back to around the lowest level since the late 1960s. Wall Street had been expecting 200,000.

#2745. Ukraine tells Putin to ‘stop complaining’ over oil refinery attacks as Russia promises retaliation

Ukrainian Foreign Minister Andrii Sybiha has told Russian President Vladimir Putin to "stop complaining" over attacks on Russian refineries, saying Putin could end Kyiv's strikes on the oil facilities by bringing an end to the Kremlin's nearly five-year-long war.

His comments come shortly after Russia's president told a meeting of government officials and senior oil executives in the Kremlin on Tuesday that Ukraine continues to attack refining facilities despite U.S. President Donald Trump's appeal to refrain from doing so.

"I would like to note that retaliatory action from our side is inevitable. Everyone must be aware of that," Putin said, according to Russian state news agency Tass.

In September, Trump called on Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy to stop targeting Russian oil refineries, saying the attacks were "hurting the world" as fuel supply disruptions from the wars in Ukraine and Iran propelled U.S. diesel prices to a record high. Moscow welcomed Trump's push for Ukraine to halt strikes on Russia's diesel-producing facilities at the time.

"If Putin doesn't like Ukraine's strikes on his oil refineries, he can order an end to the war," Ukraine's Sybiha said Tuesday via social media.

"If he doesn't want his military vessels hit, he can order an end to the war. Not to mention the 432,000 Russian soldiers he has lost on the battlefield this year without coming any closer to the goals of his invasion. Clearly, he doesn't care about them. Stop complaining. End the war," he added. CNBC could not independently verify the figures.

In July, the Center for Strategic and International Studies estimated that about 1.4 million Russian troops were killed, wounded or went missing between the start of the full-scale invasion in Feb. 2022 and June 2026, with more than 30,000 casualties a month in the first half of this year.

Ukraine has stepped up attacks on Russian critical targets in recent weeks, damaging Putin's prized Crimea bridge, striking a Russian cargo ship in the Baltic Sea and destroying one of Russia's newest ships at a Libyan port.

Kyiv has also targeted Russian energy infrastructure. Zelenskyy said earlier this month that Ukraine's long-range strikes had disabled more than 55% of Russia's oil-refining capacity, describing this as a "justified response to Russia's refusal to de-escalate and its strikes on our energy infrastructure."

Ukrainian drones reportedly struck two major Russian oil refineries in Russia's Samara region overnight, triggering fires at both facilities.

Satellite images published in late October also showed black smoke rising from multiple fires at Rosneft's Novokuibyshevsk oil refinery in southern Russia following a suspected Ukrainian drone attack.

Putin on Tuesday said Russia's fuel market situation remains "challenging" and called on government officials and oil executives "to continue reacting promptly to the supply-demand relationship for fuel across the whole territory of Russia," according to Interfax.

Ukraine's allies have warned the country faces another winter of blackouts and freezing temperatures amid escalating Russian energy strikes.

#2744. Spot-on Sucic! Croatian ace reveals Calhanoglu and Zielinski penalty inspiration after firing Inter past Stuttgart

Petar Sucic converted from the penalty spot for the second consecutive match to spark Inter's 2-0 Champions League victory over VfB Stuttgart at San Siro. Speaking after full-time, the Croatian midfielder praised injured spot-kick specialists Hakan Calhanoglu and Piotr Zielinski while highlighting Inter's improved defending against crosses.

Croatian midfielder Petar Sucic continued his clinical run from 12 yards by converting a decisive penalty to set Inter on course for a 2-0 Champions League victory over VfB Stuttgart on Wednesday night. Having also scored from the spot in Bergamo days earlier, Sucic dictated the midfield tempo at San Siro before Lautaro Martinez added a second goal to seal Inter's third straight European win.

"I managed to score from the penalty spot in two consecutive games, I'm happy to have contributed to the team's victory," Sucic said in his post-match interviews. "The group has a very strong mentality and is always very united, the season is long and we can have problems and injuries."

With regular penalty takers Hakan Calhanoglu and Piotr Zielinski sidelined through injury, Sucic stepped up once again with composure. Asked whether the absent duo had offered him tips from the spot, the midfielder hailed their pedigree while backing his own self-belief.

"If Calhanoglu and Zielinski gave me advice on penalties? They are among the best in the world, I have a lot of confidence in myself and I'm happy because I scored two goals in two games," Sucic explained.

Sucic pointed out that Inter corrected the late defensive mistakes against crosses that cost them in Bergamo, while expressing satisfaction with operating in a deep playmaking role under Cristian Chivu.

"I think we did a lot of good things in Bergamo too and were ahead, but in the end we conceded to their crosses. Stuttgart also tried today, but we were much better at defending in this situation," he noted. "Playmaker? I'm happy, I think I can play in all midfield roles and the coach used me that way too. I'm ready to give my best, I just want to help the team."

While securing an automatic top-eight finish in the Champions League league phase remains the target, Sucic urged Inter to stay grounded ahead of a demanding schedule.

"The top eight? That's the goal, but let's look at the game per game and let's see where we are," he added, with Inter next travelling to face Borussia Dortmund on Wednesday, December 9.

#2742. Berkshire breaks out to record high with fourth-quarter rally reaching 19%

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Berkshire Hathaway's red-hot shares are ending a strong week with four consecutive fresh record high closes.

Since the rally began with the start of the fourth quarter on October 1, the Class A shares have closed at record highs 11 times as they soared 19.1%.

The much more widely held B shares closed at new highs just 10 times but are also up 19.1% from their near-term low.

The gains are in sharp contrast to the S&P 500's 1.2% dip as investors appear to be seeing Berkshire as a relatively safe haven amid worries about AI stocks and spending.

Both Berkshire classes were up 3.9% on the week as the S&P advanced by 2.5%, its best week since early August.

Berkshire's B shares are now 7.5 percentage points ahead of the benchmark for the year so far.

After erasing their post-Buffett CEO announcement losses three weeks ago, Berkshire shares are now up almost 10% since last year's shareholders meeting.

They've gained around 29% since last August's lows and are up around 18% year-to-date.

Berkshire Hathaway is due to disclose its quarterly equity portfolio snapshot on Monday when it files its Q3 13F with the SEC.

We won't know for sure until then, but numbers in the company's third quarter 10-Q, released last Saturday, indicate Berkshire continued to reduce its Bank of America stake.

It would be the ninth consecutive quarter of selling.

As of June 30, Berkshire reported holding 483.4 million BAC shares valued now at $24.7 billion, it's fifth largest equity position.

On June 30, 2024, before the selling began, Berkshire owned just over 1 billion shares valued at $41.1 billion.

Since then, the number of shares in the portfolio has decreased by 53.2%, not including any Q3 sales, while the stock price has increased by 28.3%.

If there had been no reductions, the position would be worth $52.7 billion.

The belief Berkshire sold more BAC shares in the third quarter is based on a $372 million reduction in the cost basis for equities categorized by the company as "banks, insurance, and finance."

The 10-Q also reports $2.2 billion of pretax gains from equity sales.

Barron's notes Bank of America and American Express are the only financial stocks in the portfolio that have a cost basis low enough to generate a gain like that. Amex is one of the four stocks new CEO Greg Abel indicated would be held for "decades" to come in his February letter to shareholders.

In any case, Berkshire's almost 23% stake in Amex would have required it to quickly disclose any sales.

Based on the numbers in the 10-Q, the warrants Berkshire received as part of a 2011 $5 billion investment in the bank that it used in 2017 to buy 700 million shares at $7.14 each, and BAC's average stock price in the quarter, Barron's estimates 30 to 40 million shares may have been sold.

That would be roughly in line with the 30.2 million shares sold in the second quarter.

The case isn't as good, but Barron's thinks Berkshire may have bought more Alphabet shares in the period. That's based on a $2.3 billion increase in the cost basis for the category that includes Google's parent.

Overall, it looks like it will be a relatively quiet quarter with $2.4 billion in equity purchases and $2.8 billion in sales.

In the first quarter, Berkshire bought $15.9 billion and sold $24.1 billion of equities. In the second quarter, it purchased $23.5 billion and sold $3.7 billion.

All those numbers exclude the company's buybacks of its own stock.

In a term sheet filed early Friday, Berkshire Hathaway says it is selling more than 182 billion yen ($1.1 billion) worth of bonds with maturities between 2029 and 2056 and annual interest rates between 2.6% and 4.6%.

Bloomberg points out the rates, compared to benchmarks, are higher than its previous sale this year.

It quotes a fund manager as saying market sentiment "has become more sensitive," forcing issuers to make their offerings more attractive.

In April, Berkshire sold 272 billion yen ($1.7 billion) of bonds.

Berkshire uses the proceeds from the yen-denominated bonds to protect its ongoing investments in Japanese stocks from currency fluctuations.

Warren Buffett recommends Benjamin Graham's book, "The Intelligent Investor," and outlines what he considers Graham's three most important ideas.

WARREN BUFFETT: On investment books, if you're asking about that, I would recommend the first two books that Phil Fisher wrote back around 1960, "Common Sense [Stocks] and Uncommon Profits" and the second one ["Paths to Wealth Through Common Stocks"]. They're very good books.

You know, I obviously recommend, first and foremost, [Benjamin Graham's] "The Intelligent Investor," with chapters eight and 20 are the ones that you really should read.

Two of the — well, all of the important ideas in investing, really, are in that book, because there's only about three ideas. And those — two of them are emphasized in those two chapters...

One is that — to think of yourself — to think of investing as owning a business and not buying something that wiggles around in price.

And the second one is your attitude, which ties in with that, the attitude toward the market, that's covered in chapter eight. And if you have the proper attitude toward market movements, it's an enormous help in securities.

And the final chapter is on the margin of safety, which means, don't try and drive a 9,800-pound truck over a bridge that says it's, you know, "Capacity: 10,000 pounds." But go down the road a little bit and find one that says, "Capacity: 15,000 pounds."

Berkshire market capitalization: $1,259,897,054,755

Berkshire Cash as of Sept 30: $377.4 billion (Up 3.3% from June 30)

Excluding Rail Cash and Subtracting T-Bills Payable: $352.3 billion (Down 1.9% from June 30)

Berkshire repurchased $9.6 billion of its shares in Q3 2026.

Berkshire's top holdings of disclosed publicly traded stocks in the U.S. and Japan, by market value, based on the latest closing prices.

Holdings are as of June 30, 2026, as reported in Berkshire Hathaway's 13F filing on August 14, 2026, except for:

#2741. Liesman: Why Greenspan could bet on productivity — and Warsh needs to hike

In a discussion on CNBC's Halftime Report Wednesday, the question came up: Why can't Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh hold the line on interest rates the way his icon Alan Greenspan did at the beginning of the dot-com era?

Greenspan famously leaned against inflation hawks on his committee in 1995 and 1996, holding the line on interest rates amid strong growth, because he believed in the productivity promise of the internet revolution.

It would be nice if Warsh could follow the same playbook amid the artificial intelligence build-out and keep rates steady until the productivity arrives. Unfortunately, a quick look at the numbers shows just how different the situation is for Warsh when it comes to inflation, the magnitude of the capital-investment boom and the monetary and fiscal stimulus boosting the economy.

Bottom line: the Fed looks like it has to hike — and maybe substantially — to be anywhere close to the position where the Fed was in Greenspan's time at the dawn of a major technological revolution.

It may be that the massive capex spending on AI will mean more productivity and less inflation down the road. But the front-end build-out is proving to be more inflationary, and more challenging, to a Fed already dealing with high inflation. In many ways, that's the opposite of what Greenspan had to handle.

#2740. What to know: Cricket's Sandpapergate wounds reopen as teammates exchange blame

BRISBANE, Australia (AP) — Cricket is imploding again 8 1/2 years after the cheating scandal dubbed "Sandpapergate."

Longtime Australia teammates are calling each other out over their conflicting versions of what happened. There have been accusations of scapegoating, of lies and cover-ups. Pot shots are coming from outside and within the inner-sanctum.

Three Australian players received unprecedented bans over a plot to illegally tamper with the ball during a test match — the traditional, five-day version of cricket — in March 2018 against South Africa in what was already an acrimonious series.

An autobiography published by retired batter Usman Khawaja in early November alleged that everyone in the Australia squad and staff knew of the sandpaper plan but let captain Steve Smith, vice-captain David Warner and Cameron Bancroft — who was literally caught red-handed — take the fall. Smith and Warner accepted 12-month bans from Cricket Australia and returned in 2019. Bancroft was suspended for nine months, and never re-established his spot in the Australian team.

Warner and Bancroft, among others, supported Khawaja's claim. Pat Cummins, the incumbent Australia captain who was among the bowlers in that match, described Khawaja's claim as "complete and utter nonsense." His three longtime partners in Australia's so-called bowling "cartel " have also distanced themselves from the plot.

The initial Cricket Australia investigation, conducted within a matter of days, leveled the blame on three members of the team. Questions are being raised now about why all players, coaches and support staff weren't interviewed as part of the investigation.

Some critics have torched Khawaja's decision to reopen the biggest scandal in decades in Australian cricket and questioned his motives. Backers have praised Khawaja's courage for speaking out and acknowledging the win-at-all-costs mentality that typified the team of that era, even if he went public only after retiring. Khawaja said the Australians should have accountability as a team.

Intense media coverage has renewed focus on Warner's central role in the sandpaper plot. Bancroft, for the first time publicly, stated it was Warner's idea.

Just like their counterparts in baseball, cricket players are capable of devising devious ways of getting extra movement on a thrown ball. It's sometimes known as "ball management." What made Sandpapergate such a scandal was the sheer brazenness of it.

Tampering usually involves either making one half of the ball rough or unpicking the stitches in the seam. Teeth, fingernails, bottle caps, zippers, dirt and grit have been used to gouge the ball. Wet candy, mints, gum and glue have been used on the cricket ball. Saliva was typically used to shine one side of the ball — to make it swing through the air — until that was banned in 2020 during the health crisis of the COVID-19 era.

Some famous players — it's a long list but here are a few names, all former captains: Mike Atherton of England, Sachin Tendulkar of India, Faf du Plessis of South Africa — have been accused of or sanctioned by the International Cricket Council for ball tampering. Their punishments amounted to fines or comparatively minor suspensions.

The abrasive sheets of paper have always been used to rub the face and edges of cricket bats, which are made of willow. Cummins says there's still sandpaper in the locker rooms to use on bats. He said it was also used in practice sessions to scuff the outside surface of the leather balls so that batters could prepare to face deliveries that swing through the air unconventionally.

But using it on the field, during the game, that's extraordinary. And that's what got the Australian team into this prolonged mess.

Some wonder why this case was treated so harshly. Cricket Australia administrators recognized the sport's long-established status in the national consciousness, and realized the Sandpapergate scandal brought the game into disrepute and shattered the team's standing in the community.

Michael Kasprowicz, a former test fast bowler and Cricket Australia director, told Australian Associated Press that the hard line taken was probably more about perception than tampering.

"I know from a board point of view at the time, it wasn't about damaging the ball. It was about damaging the brand," he said.

Bancroft was the initial face in the scandal, after South African television broadcast footage of him with the sandpaper on the field.

He published a roughly 4,500-word account of his recollections after Khawaja, Warner and Cummins made public statements.

"It was not just the three of us ... It was the whole team," Bancroft said. He felt "freed" after finally telling his story, he said, adding that the "final straw" was a news conference staged by Cummins that "made me feel angry, betrayed and alone."

Warner responded to the initial fallout from Khawaja's book by supporting his longtime friend and saying Cricket Australia's investigation into the scandal was "rushed and botched. "

But Cummins, in a 70-minute stand-up news conference, accused Khawaja of being "casual" and "flippant."

"When he says we all knew, if by that he means that we knew sandpaper was going out in the field, he's 100% wrong," Cummins said.

In a clearout after the 2018 series, head coach Darren Lehmann, who has denied any knowledge of the plot, was replaced by Justin Langer, who was succeeded by Andrew McDonald in 2022. Veteran wicketkeeper Tim Paine replaced Smith as test captain before Cummins took over in 2021.

Cricket Australia has a new CEO, Todd Greenberg, who has ruled out reopening the Sandpapergate investigation but plans to establish an independent forum for the players. He said the sport's administrators aren't taking any side of the debate.

Former Australia captain Greg Chappell, who was embroiled in the so-called "Underarm" controversy in the 1980s, wants the "bickering" to stop.

"What worries me is that it's dividing people who love the same game," he wrote in a column for cricinfo.com, "and I fear it will open chasms inside a team that has to take the field together."

→ Baca artikel asli

#2739. Austin Reaves talks for first time ahead of Spurs clash: ‘Felt like I broke my foot’

Austin Reaves, who has missed the last five games because of a bruised left heel, was a full participant in Lakers practice on Wednesday but his status remains unknown for Friday's game against the Spurs.

"It felt good to play basketball again," Reaves said in his first public comments since suffering the injury Nov. 11. "Wind was a little bad, but kind of to be expected being out two weeks. But felt good. You know, we'll see how I wake up [Thursday]. We're still going in the right direction, but felt really good today."

Austin Reaves, left, and Luka Doncic talk during a game against the Utah Jazz in Los Angeles, Wednesday, Nov. 18, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

Reaves sustained the injury after landing awkwardly following a missed fast-break dunk in the third quarter of a game against the Bucks.

He said he's not quite sure what happened on the play.

"No, I had no idea," he said. "Tried to play through it, and then I drove baseline, planted on it, and was like, I can't do this. So walked to the back and was in some pretty bad pain. I didn't think I broke my foot, but it sure felt like I broke my foot."

Lakers coach JJ Redick, who suffered a similar injury in April 2016, has repeatedly described the heel bruise as a "pain tolerance issue." Reaves echoed that Wednesday.

"It's not a strain in your hamstring or something like that," Reaves said. "It's kind of just something that's going to linger around if you continue to play before you're ready. And basically, the main message was, let's get this back to normal before we put you back out there and put yourself at risk again so later on in the season you won't have to deal with a flare up or something."

The Lakers went 4-1 after Reaves suffered the injury, but they clearly missed his offensive production after falling to the Jazz on Nov. 18, 109-97, and barely squeaking past the Hornets (118-114) and Kings (106-104). Reaves is averaging 24.7 points (51.3% shooting from the field, 40.9% from beyond the arc), 3.3 rebounds and 4.7 assists.

"One of those things that just, it sucks," Reaves said. "You know, I was rolling, playing well. We were rolling as a team, wanted to continue to be out there and play games. Hate missing games. But it's one of those things, like I said, there wasn't much that we could really do. But we took care of business."

Austin Reaves #15 of the Los Angeles Lakers reacts during a game against the Utah Jazz at Crypto.com Arena on Monday, Nov. 23, 2026, in Los Angeles. NBAE via Getty Images

When asked how Reaves looked Wednesday, Redick didn't hesitate.

When reporters prodded him to see if he was being sarcastic, he doubled down, "Yeah, not joking," he added.

Austin Reaves (15) of the Los Angeles Lakers smiles during a game against the Utah Jazz at Crypto.com Arena in Los Angeles on Wednesday, Nov. 18, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

The Lakers sure hope that Reaves can return Friday and that Redick is right considering the challenge they have ahead of them.

The Lakers and Spurs are tied for the best record in the NBA at 15-3 and Luka Doncic and Victor Wembanyama are currently in a two-man race for the MVP Award.

When asked if it's important to him to return against the Spurs, Reaves balked.

"I mean, I want to play," Reaves said. "…I didn't get hurt the night of Milwaukee and look at the schedule and be like, 'God, I need to be back before the San Antonio game.' If I'm back, good, we'll go to war. But I got to listen to my body and take care of myself. But it should be fun if I'm healthy and get out there."

#2738. Here's a roadmap to the Aaron Rodgers developments, one of the craziest stories in years

If you were traveling on Wednesday or otherwise not plugged in for one of the most rollicking, unpredictable days in years if not ever, you're in luck.

We've reconstructed the full timeline as to the events regarding now-former Steelers quarterback Aaron Rodgers.

It actually started on Monday, when coach Mike McCarthy was asked whether he has considered using one of the team's other three quarterbacks when the offense is struggling. McCarthy, whose body language in hindsight suggests he was dreading the question, said without equivocation, "Aaron Rodgers is our quarterback."

On Tuesday, Rodgers took his usual reps at practice. He conducted his usual midweek press conference. Unbeknownst to most of the media or the general public, the Steelers were changing their play-calling procedures to have quarterbacks coach Tom Arth, and not McCarthy, communicate the calls to Rodgers through the speaker in his helmet.

Then came Tuesday night. At 11:10 p.m. ET, the Steelers issued a statement — not from coach Mike McCarthy or G.M. Omar Khan or owner Art Rooney II but from team spokesman Burt Lauten — that Mason Rudolph will start on Friday against the Broncos and Rodgers will be inactive.

The news forced the Broncos to make a quick pivot in their preparations from Rodgers to Rudolph. As one source put it, the Broncos weren't sure whether it was a good thing or a bad thing.

It was definitely a bad thing, all things considered, for the Steelers.

By Wednesday morning, reports emerged that it was a coach's decision. At 8:00 a.m. ET, McCarthy informed the team of the decision.

Rodgers wasn't there. As it turns out, he was excused.

Enter Pat McAfee. He teased a surprise guest at 1:40 p.m. ET. As expected, it was Rodgers.

Rodgers took a flamethrower to McCarthy, airing out dirty laundry that created a major mess for his now-former team. He exposed the fact that, at halftime of the Week 11 loss to the Eagles, McCarthy broke down the locker-room gathering by saying, "1-2-3 Packers."

Rodgers also said McCarthy was falling asleep during some meetings and missing other meetings, expressing (in passive-aggressive fashion) concern for McCarthy's health.

But Rodgers also said it was nothing new, and that McCarthy had done the same things in Green Bay, when they worked there together a decade ago.

Rodgers also made it clear he wouldn't stay with the Steelers. After his session with McAfee and company, Dulac reported that the Steelers were expected to release Rodgers. By the official close of business, they'd waived him.

A question lingers as to whether the Steelers will try to deny Rodgers the balance of his $22 million salary. It won't be easy; they'll need to both void the guarantees and wipe out his eligibility for termination pay (in the form of his remaining salary) under the Collective Bargaining Agreement.

McCarthy met with reporters on Wednesday, after all hell had broken loose. He admitted to the "1-2-3 Packers" comment, calling it an "[e]mbarrassing moment."

McCarthy also said that Rodgers was benched after issuing an "ultimatum" that was "clearly outside of his job description."

ESPNFL thereafter reported that McCarthy "has the full support" of the organization, and that the team is "not planning to make any immediate changes." The key word may be "immediate"; with few viable options only two days before a Black Friday game against the Broncos, changes could come later.

The overriding takeaway is that someone with the Steelers knew or should have known that McCarthy and Rodgers couldn't co-exist. Because, obviously, they didn't co-exist.

If, as Rodgers claims, McCarthy believed Rodgers got him fired in Green Bay, McCarthy shouldn't have wanted a reunion with Rodgers. And if, as Rodgers claims, McCarthy was doing the same kinds of things in Green Bay that he did in Pittsburgh, Rodgers shouldn't have wanted a reunion with McCarthy.

The reunion has now ended abruptly, with seven games left in the 2026 regular season. It remains to be seen whether Rodgers wants to play with another team, or whether another team wants him. Any team that signs him risks being on the receiving end of a future gripe session from Rodgers in which he lists all the things that team and/or the coach did wrong — without ever acknowledging his own role in the reality that it didn't work out.

The good news, if there is any, is this: If Rodgers never plays again, his final NFL pass will have been a touchdown.

Which is a better outcome than if he'd retired after the 2025 season. His final pass that year was a pick-six in the playoffs.

#2737. The Lakers’ wing depth, adaptability are becoming strengths this season

Entering 2026-27, perhaps the most consequential unknown resided on the wings for the Los Angeles Lakers. They knew they could count on Luka Dončić and Austin Reaves to provide All-Star-caliber (and better) production in the backcourt. They were banking on Walker Kessler, at worst, providing high-level rim protection and interior play-finishing, based on his tenure with the Utah Jazz.

Filling in the gaps at small forward and power forward, as well as minutes off the bench at those spots, was a more dubious proposition. Jake LaRavia was coming off a very poor second half in 2025-26. Matisse Thybulle had battled injuries in recent years while Ziaire Williams was yet to establish firm footing through five NBA seasons. Quentin Grimes' role and performance had been a little sporadic the past few seasons.

Yet five weeks into 2026-27, all of them are donning different caps and playing darn good basketball for the 15-3 Lakers — the product of a team-first, resilient mindset.

"I think they're all really grateful to be here, to be Lakers. They're all really grateful to be on this team and be part of the group. I know [Matisse] has expressed that to me on a couple of occasions. For [Ziaire], Matisse, [Quentin], they've experienced adversity in their careers in different ways," head coach JJ Redick said after Monday's win over the Utah Jazz." I think that gratitude piece is like…they're just all in a situation where they're putting the team first and they're putting their egos aside. That stuff matters. I think it's one of the reasons our group has had early success because we said on day one, we wanted this season to be about the team and not any one individual one of us and, so far, it's been that."

Averaging 2.3 steals and 1.0 blocks per game while shooting 40.5% from deep, Thybulle has been a defensive menace and knocked down open 3s when the ball swings his way. Williams has assumed a lot of lofty point-of-attack duties and excelled as a spot-up shooter (42.1% beyond the arc) and cutter (73.5% on 2-pointers).

Grimes scaled up his offensive role while Reaves was sidelined for two weeks and recorded three 20-point outings over that span (including a career-high 47 against the Charlotte Hornets). He's also shooting 40.3% from deep. LaRavia has rebounded from a bumpy end to last season to shoot 43.2% beyond the arc, relentlessly attack the offensive glass and be a reliable help defender.

Dončić, Reaves and Kessler may be leading the way but Los Angeles wouldn't have charged to the top of the league without their versatile quartet on the wings.

"I think just everyone is willing to sacrifice for the same goal. We just have a lot of adaptable players, I think," Jake LaRavia told reporters postgame on Monday. "A lot of it's lineup-based, matchup-based, so whoever the coaches think should start that night is who's going to start. I think, as players, we're all willing to be kind of thrown in and out of the lineup and it doesn't really change much for us as players. You just keep the same mindset and keep the same goal and just go out there and play hard."

#2734. Chicago Bears vs. Detroit Lions: Picks and Predictions

On Thanksgiving at Ford Field, the 8-2 Bears travel to face the 8-2 Lions in a battle for sole possession of first place in the NFC North. It doesn't get any better than this, folks. Chicago arrives on a five-game winning streak behind Caleb Williams and a physical rushing attack that has slowed somewhat in the last few weeks with the loss of Cole Kmet, while Detroit is rolling on a six-game run with Jared Goff and one of the league's most explosive offenses. On defense, the Bears are as healthy as they have been all season, minus Kyler Gordon, and will feature Big Jeff Simmons for the first time against the Detroit offense.

While the Lions defense has gotten back Branch and Joseph in their secondary, they have lost two Pro Bowl players in Jack Campbell and Aidan Hutchinson to season-ending injuries. #2733. SpaceX wins FCC approval to deploy 1 million orbital data center satellites

The Federal Communications Commission has authorized Elon Musk's SpaceX to put 1 million artificial intelligence computing satellites in orbit, Chairman Brendan Carr announced on Wednesday.

Carr said in a post on X, the social network owned by SpaceX, that the company's Starmind constellation, and "competitor constellations will use solar power to extend America's lead" in AI.

Musk said in a reply, "Much appreciated. This will be a great asset to America and the world."

The FCC, in its authorization filing, noted that SpaceX's application "received a substantial number of comments from the public, including petitions to deny filed by Kuiper Systems LLC (Amazon Leo), Viasat Inc. (Viasat), and the American Astronomical Society (AAS)."

In a March petition urging the FCC to deny SpaceX's application, Amazon had labeled the company's Starmind project as a "lofty ambition rather than a real plan."

The e-commerce and cloud computing giant is building a competitor to SpaceX's Starlink satellite internet service, and said it would take SpaceX "centuries" to build the constellation, adding that the company provided scant details about how it would "deliver on these grand claims."

The petition resulted in a rebuke from Carr, who said Amazon "should focus on the fact" that it's falling short of its own satellite deployment milestone.

Amazon declined to comment on the FCC's approval.

Other companies have also set their sights on orbital data centers. Google last year announced Project Suncatcher, which it describes as a "moonshot to put AI in space," and launched its first chips into space on a SpaceX rocket and Planet Labs satellite in October.

In March, Jeff Bezos' Blue Origin petitioned the FCC to let it launch up to 51,600 satellites as part of a low Earth orbit satellite constellation dubbed "Project Sunrise." The constellation would work in tandem with Blue Origin's planned TeraWave satellite internet service, announced in January.

Astrophysicist and author Adam Becker was critical of the FCC move in an email to CNBC on Wednesday.

"Any serious attempt at this is going to further pollute the night sky and runs a serious risk of a cascading chain reaction of space junk collisions, which would be a disaster for all of humanity," he wrote. "Musk and Carr are either profoundly stupid themselves, or hoping the rest of us are too stupid to notice this is nonsense."

SpaceX did not respond to a request for comment.

A senior attorney with Earthjustice, Jan Hasselman, criticized the FCC for issuing its decision on a holiday week, and approving the SpaceX proposal without any environmental review, which he said was required by the National Environmental Policy Act.

The FCC said in its authorization that it dismissed "comments and concerns raised regarding topics over which the Commission has no authority, including environmental concerns," and concerns about its impact on "optical astronomy."

#2732. ‘Ready for any storm’: UAE minister optimistic on economy as IMF lifts forecast

The UAE is "very optimistic" about achieving 7%-8% growth next year, the country's economy and tourism minister Abdulla bin Touq Al Marri told CNBC after the latest data from the International Monetary Fund showed the country would avoid a recession in 2026.

The IMF last week revised the UAE's real GDP forecast to 0.2% growth in 2026 from a 3.9% contraction predicted a month ago. "The UAE is coming back, and that adjustment within one month shows that there is a huge potential aspect when it comes towards the end of the year," the minister told CNBC's Dan Murphy.

He said the tourism industry was already picking up with occupancy rates returning to "almost near normal" and international carriers including British Airways and Air France rerouting flights to the UAE. The minister expects economic growth to be higher than 0.2% this year.

The Iran war has disrupted economic activity in the UAE, with missile and drone attacks from Tehran and maritime disruptions weighing on energy trade, tourism, aviation and private-sector confidence.

Earlier this month, U.S. President Donald Trump said that renewed attacks on Iran were "highly unlikely" if negotiations produce a "very strong agreement." However, on Monday, Trump threatened that Tehran could face fresh U.S. strikes next year if the two countries failed to strike a deal, as per Fox News.

"We can we cannot promise the world for any storms that will land on our shore, but we can always promise that the UAE's economy is ready for any storm," Al Marri said.

He added that next year the UAE's economy will have a "different" model than the one before the conflict and investments will be focused on building factories and "things that we really need in times of crisis."

The World Bank in October said it expects the UAE's economy to recover next year, driven by rising oil production and exports and a rebound in tourism, trade, transportation, financial services, and construction.

It warned that the improvement in economic activity in the UAE largely depends on the duration and intensity of the Iran war, with a faster de-escalation likely to accelerate the recovery in services exports and private-sector activity.

#2729. JJ Redick Calls Out ESPN’s Overreactions To Lakers: ‘We’re Either Contenders Or A Fake Team’

Ahead of the Los Angeles Lakers' upcoming fixture against the San Antonio Spurs, their head coach JJ Redick spoke to the media at the post-practice scrum at the training facility, where he indirectly addressed Stephen A. Smith and Ramona Shelburne's recent criticism of the Lakers.

When a reporter asked him about his thoughts ahead of facing a top team in the Western Conference, Redick pointed out that their plans are not just focused on facing one team on this road trip but on several top teams in the West.

"This entire road trip is a massive challenge for us. Four games, and all of them are going to present different tests. We're playing against the Western Conference Finals champion from last year, who are off to a great start," he said.

"Then you get Memphis, Houston, and New Orleans. So, there's a lot on this trip. It's not just one game. I think these four games will be a great barometer of where we're at."

"But to your point, whether it's guys being in and out of the lineup, changes in playing style, tweaks we make, or rotation changes, things are always evolving."

"Whether we lose a game or win a game, we're either going to be contenders on [ESPN's] First Take, or we're not going to be contenders, and we're fake. The overreactions game-to-game are what they are. That stuff is just noise. We're going to focus on the process of trying to get better. It's a good test to see where we're at right now," Redick concluded on the subject.

Stephen A. Smith, who is the executive producer of ESPN's First Take and also appears often on the show, has recently claimed that the Lakers do not have the right foundational core to compete for a championship.

In simple terms, he believes that since no team has won a championship with a core of white players in the modern NBA, the Lakers do not have NBA history on their side to compete for a ring this season without adding more Black players to their roster.

Moreover, Ramona Shelburne also appeared on ESPN and claimed that the Lakers' recent form was not a reliable sample size to test their performance since she believed they had one of the easiest schedules in the NBA until that point.

While shutting down all the media narratives as 'noise,' JJ Redick has emphasized the fragile and extreme nature of such media narratives that swing very easily, especially considering the subject is the Lakers, one of the most storied franchises in NBA history.

He did not directly name Stephen A. Smith or Ramona Shelburne because it seems he did not want to engage with him directly over this. But it is clear that the Lakers are locked in on letting their performance on the court do the talking.

At 15-3, the Lakers are second in the Western Conference and riding a two-game winning streak. However, their upcoming four-game road trip against the Spurs, Grizzlies, Rockets, and Pelicans will be a significant test.

If they navigate this stretch successfully, they can strengthen their championship credentials and silence critics. And with Austin Reaves expected to be back in the next game, it has certainly boosted their chances of doing so.

But it remains to be seen if JJ Redick and the Lakers can prove the media critics wrong.

#2728. Nvidia has lost $2 trillion in value. Here's what options traders expect from earnings

Jensen Huang's forced exit from Nvidia shocked Wall Street and Washington, wiped $2 trillion off its market value, and raised serious questions about national security and regulation of the AI complex. Traders, for the moment, must content themselves with the comparatively mundane business of Tuesday's earnings, and options traders are expecting big moves.

With volatility in the stock near the highest since April 2025, options traders are now pricing a 7% reaction in the stock after its report, the biggest implied move going into an Nvidia report since February 2025, and well above the median move of 3.2% over the last eight quarters.

Implied volatility reached 68 Monday, compared to under 40 in early October, according to ThinkOrSwim data. Nvidia vol reached a multi-year high above 92 in the aftermath of President Trump's tariff Liberation day in April '25. In other words, the company that for much of the AI boom was the most reliable winner in the market, is now more volatile than all but 35 companies in the S&P 500.

"Longer term, I see significant downside risk unless Nvidia can attract an exceptional leader, someone like Tim Cook or AMD's Lisa Su," Nigam Arora, founder of the Arora report, said in a text ahead of the company's after-market report on Tuesday.

Since hitting all-time highs in October, Nvidia shares have fallen 35%, shedding $2 trillion in market capitalization along the way. Including Nvidia, there are only five companies in the S&P 500 worth more than $2 trillion. However, there is evidence that bulls have been holding out hope while bears snatch up profits: the ratio of open puts to call contracts has actually widened towards calls, according to Barchart data. The ratio is currently 0.87, down from a one-year high of 1.02 two weeks ago and the lowest since the first week of October.

The most popular contract by volume on Monday was the 155-strike put expiring at the bell Monday, but the most popular trade for later expiry was the 160-strike call expiring Thursday, and the second-most-common purchase was the 190-strike call expiring Friday, SpotGamma data show.

One catch is that most call contracts traded Monday looked like neutral to bearish positions opened by traders selling calls. Of the 5 million options traded on Monday, 3 million were calls, but more were likely opened by sellers than buyers. Of the $2.3 billion in options premium traded on Nvidia Monday, $1.2 billion was tied to puts, SpotGamma data show.

With bears taking profits in puts the past month, there's another potential snag on open interest. There are very few open put contracts at or below the $157 level at which the stock closed Monday. That suggests any bad surprise would find many traders with little downside protection.

By the same token, there's notable open interest in call contracts from the 175-strike and above, suggesting profit-taking on a bounce could limit Nvidia's recovery past levels it breached over the past week.

Either way, Tuesday's earnings could provide fireworks.

#2726. Hedge funds are no longer just for the rich. They’re wooing everyday investors

For decades, hedge funds were the preserve of billionaires, pension funds and deep-pocketed institutional investors. Now, the industry is increasingly courting a new pool of capital: everyday investors.

The shift, dubbed by some as the "retailization" of hedge funds, comes as alternative asset managers seek new avenues for growth and investors look for ways to shield their portfolios from increasingly volatile equity markets.

Unlike traditional equity funds, which typically rely on stocks rising to generate returns, hedge funds can employ strategies including short selling, arbitrage and derivatives to seek profits across different market conditions.

For an industry already managing record levels of assets, individual investors represent a largely untapped growth opportunity, experts who spoke to CNBC said.

"Hedge funds are entrepreneurs. Their objective is to make as much money as they possibly can, both in returns and for their organization," said Don Steinbrugge, CEO of hedge fund consulting firm Agecroft Partners.

"So if they have the ability to go after a new market and grow their business, they're going to do that."

The push is coming from both hedge fund managers seeking to broaden their investor base and traditional asset managers looking to capitalize on growing demand for alternative investments, said Ken Heinz, president of hedge fund research firm HFR.

"Heightened geopolitical uncertainty, greater market volatility, inflation concerns, and the higher correlation between equities and bonds have raised questions about the effectiveness of conventional portfolio diversification," said Ricky Siao, a hedge fund specialist at Swiss private bank UBP.

The investment environment has become more challenging for investors who have historically relied on traditional allocations to equities and fixed income, he added.

As a result, investors are increasingly seeking exposure to strategies such as arbitrage, event-driven investing and quantitative macro strategies to reduce their dependence on equity market returns.

The democratization of hedge fund investing is taking shape through a growing array of investment vehicles, often with lower fees, smaller minimum investments and more frequent opportunities to withdraw money.

Some of the biggest names in the hedge fund industry are already moving in that direction.

Billionaire investor Bill Ackman's Pershing Square raised $5 billion in April for a publicly listed closed-end fund, giving everyday investors access to its investment expertise without the performance fees traditionally associated with hedge funds. The fund charges a 2% annual management fee.

British hedge fund giant Man Group, meanwhile, entered the U.S. ETF market last September and doubled its lineup to four actively managed funds just three months later.

The broader shift is also changing how hedge fund strategies are packaged for individual investors.

"What has changed most is the wrapper, not the strategy itself," said Yi Ling Ong, managing partner and chief operating officer at Golden Horse Fund Management.

In other words, hedge fund strategies are increasingly being packaged in more accessible vehicles such as mutual funds and ETFs, often with lower investment minimums and fewer restrictions on withdrawals.

"Investors may still be getting exposure to long/short equity, credit, or macro strategies, but delivered through vehicles that offer quarterly or monthly liquidity and standardized reporting."

In Europe, hedge fund managers have adapted strategies to fit regulated UCITS funds, which are accessible to individual investors. In the U.S., mutual funds and ETFs have similarly broadened access to alternative investment strategies.

In Asia, digital wealth platforms are helping bridge the gap between institutional and individual investors.

Singapore-based Endowus, which began offering hedge funds on its platform in 2022, has lowered minimum investments for semi-liquid funds to $50,000, while removing minimums altogether for some daily-liquid alternative funds, according to Chief Investment Officer Hugh Chung.

While most hedge funds on the platform remain restricted to accredited investors, a growing number of alternative funds are becoming available to non-accredited investors, Chung said.

India is seeing a similar shift through Specialized Investment Funds, or SIFs, which allow investors to access more sophisticated strategies through structures that more closely resemble traditional mutual funds, said Rajesh Singla of Alpha AMC.

Assets under management in India's Specialized Investment Funds have more than quadrupled since March to 426.7 billion rupees as of end-October, according to data from the Association of Mutual Funds in India.

The products carry a minimum investment of 1 million rupees, significantly below the entry thresholds typically associated with traditional hedge funds.

The shift is also making hedge fund-style investing cheaper.

Traditional hedge funds have long been associated with the "2 and 20" fee model, which typically charges a 2% annual management fee and taking 20% of investment profits.

While those fees have come under pressure over the years, retail-friendly alternatives such as hedge fund-like ETFs typically charge a single annual management fee of between 0.4% and 1.25%, according to Ong.

But lower fees does not necessarily mean better outcomes.

Retail investment vehicles often face tighter regulatory and liquidity requirements, which can limit managers' ability to replicate strategies used by traditional hedge funds.

Steinbrugge said those constraints can force managers to avoid less liquid securities that may offer some of the greatest opportunities for outsized returns.

"What investors are really choosing between is not just price, but structure," Ong said.

The industry's expansion into the retail market is also raising questions about whether individual investors fully understand the complexity of the strategies they are buying into.

Easier access does not eliminate the risks associated with leverage, short selling or illiquid investments.

"The key risk is that investors may underestimate the complexity and risk associated with these strategies simply because they are now more accessible," Singla said.

Liquidity is another concern, particularly for less liquid alternative investments being marketed through retail-friendly structures.

Some interval funds, which are increasingly being used to distribute private credit and other alternative investments to individuals, may offer to repurchase only 5% of their outstanding shares each quarter, Steinbrugge said.

That could leave investors unable to withdraw as much money as they want during periods of market stress.

Still, industry watchers largely see the rise of retail-friendly products as complementary to traditional hedge funds rather than an existential threat to them.

While the proliferation of more liquid, transparent and lower-fee products could intensify competition and pressure traditional fee structures, Siao does not expect retailization to fundamentally reshape the industry in the foreseeable future.

"I would expect this to be an incremental evolution rather than a fundamental transformation of the hedge fund business model," he said.

"Over time, the industry will likely remain segmented," Ong said. "Retail vehicles will address accessibility and scale, while hedge funds will continue to focus on depth, flexibility, and outcomes that are difficult to replicate in simplified formats."

#2725. Kantserov Keeps Rolling, Blackhawks Beat Kraken For Perfect Road Trip

Rookie Roman Kantserov extended his goal streak to three games with another two tallies, as the Chicago Blackhawks (9-14-2) defeated the Seattle Kraken (11-10-1) 3-1 at Climate Pledge Arena on Wednesday.

Kantserov now leads the Blackhawks and all NHL rookies with 15 goals and 20 points this season, scoring six times and recording eight points during the team's perfect 3-0 Northwestern road trip. The Russian winger is also sixth in the NHL in goals, ahead of notables like Connor McDavid and Auston Matthews.

After Cole Smith opened the scoring early in the third period, Kantserov tallied the eventual game-winner less than two minutes later, firing a one-timer from a sharp angle into Seattle's net on the power play. The Blackhawks have now scored on the power play in seven straight games and have converted on 11 of 20 attempts during that stretch.

Kantserov added his second goal with an empty-netter to seal the win in the final seconds.

Patrick Kane extended his point streak to six games with an assist on Kantserov's power-play strike, but unfortunately, his night ended not too long after.

With the extra attacker on the ice for the Kraken, Kane blocked a shot in the defensive zone and dropped to the ice, clearly in pain and unable to put much pressure on his right skate. He immediately went to the locker room and did not return for the final 3:01.

Seattle snapped Spencer Knight's shutout bid with a late goal from Jordan Eberle, but otherwise, the 25-year-old was flawless on Wednesday, turning aside 29 of 30 shots. It was arguably Knight's best performance of the season. He was credited with 1.98 goals saved above expected.

Knight outdueled Kraken starter Joey Daccord, who suffered just his second loss in eight career games against the Blackhawks. Daccord stopped all 11 shots faced in the first two periods, but got beat twice in the third. He allowed two goals on 19 shots across 60 minutes.

When the Blackhawks boarded the plane for Calgary last week to begin their road trip, they hadn't won away from the United Center in their first 22 games this season. They'll now return to Chicago with three road wins in their pocket after sweeping the bottom of the Pacific Division.

Here are highlights from the Blackhawks 3-1 win in Seattle, moving to 5-1-1 in their last seven games and 9-14-2 in the 2026-27 regular season.

Knight was up to an early task, denying Mackie Samoskevich on a penalty shot just 1:02 into the game. Ian Cole and Artyom Levshunov collided at their own blue line, and Samoskevich took advantage of the open ice for a breakaway. Kantserov hooked Samoskevich on the backcheck, but no harm, no foul. Still scoreless.

#2723. South Korea upgrades growth forecast, raises benchmark rates to highest in two years

South Korea's central bank on Thursday hiked benchmark lending rates to their highest in two years, as inflation continues to run above target while a booming economy allows it room to tighten policy.

The central bank raised rates by 25 basis points to 3.25% for its final meeting of the year, in line with expectations from economists, data on the Bank of Korea's website showed.

Inflation is expected to remain above its 2% target level for a considerable time, the BOK added in its statement, saying this was due to the pass-through of elevated cost pressures and as demand-side pressures also gradually strengthen, driven by stronger economic growth. Inflation in the country stood at 2.8% in October.

The BOK also upgraded its growth forecasts for 2026 to 3.9% for 2026 and 4.1% for 2027, up from the 3.3% and 2.9%, respectively, forecast in August.

South Korea's third-quarter GDP grew by 3.4% year on year, beating expectations, allowing the central bank room to raise rates.

"The projections of high growth rates for next year and beyond also reflect the significantly increasing weight of the IT sector as a growth engine for the Korean economy," BOK Governor Shin Hyun-song said at a press conference following the decision.

Operating profits in the semiconductor sector could reach 700 trillion won, Shin told reporters, according to local news agency Yonhap.

Shin also signaled that more rate hikes, although he did not give a fixed timeline.

"While the domestic economy is expected to continue its robust growth, inflation is forecast to remain above the target level for a considerable time ... The Board, therefore, sees it warranted to continue its policy response until there is confidence that inflation will converge stably to the target level."

Gareth Leather, senior Asia Economist at Capital Economics said there is reason to believe that the BOK's tightening cycle was drawing to a close.

The BOK has raised rates by 75 basis points in five months, as higher energy prices from the Iran war and the AI boom has pushed up inflation in the country.

Leather said that the country's strong growth, as well as a strengthening won could ease concerns over inflation.

While rapidly rising property prices could strengthen the case for a prolonged tightening cycle, Leather said that the BOK was "more likely to favour macroprudential measures over further rate hikes to address its financial stability concerns."

After the decision, the country's benchmark Kospi rose 1.88%.

#2722. Kings revert to struggling ways vs. short-handed teams after signs of promise

Kings revert to struggling ways vs. short-handed teams after signs of promise originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

It appeared the Sacramento Kings had turned a corner in their miserable season last weekend. Then they reverted.

They had a spirited performance in a loss to the West-leading Los Angeles Lakers last Friday, with Zach LaVine just missing a game-winning 3-pointer at the buzzer. Then they beat the struggling Boston Celtics on Saturday while limiting one of the best teams in the East to 34-percent shooting overall.

The difference in intensity from Sacramento in those two games jumped off the floor, and it looked like the Kings might have been pulling out of their early-season malaise.

Fast forward to Wednesday, and the Kings were back to looking like a team headed straight to the draft lottery's relegation zone with one of the NBA's three worst records.

The Kings lost Monday to the San Antonio Spurs while they were missing three typical starters, including former Sacramento point guard De'Aaron Fox and all-world center Victor Wembanyama.

The Kings lost by 16. Then they traveled down Interstate 80 to San Francisco and fell to the Stephen Curry-less Golden State Warriors on Wednesday, 129-110, after falling behind by 25 points in the first quarter.

Sacramento is now 2-16 at Thanksgiving. And beyond getting second-year center Maxime Raynaud back from a knee injury soon, reinforcements don't appear to be coming.

"It's tough. We've dug ourselves a super deep hole," Keegan Murray said Wednesday. "So obviously, it's tough to figure out where my head's at. We just gotta figure out how to win games.

Sacramento outscored Golden State by 13 points combined through the second and third quarters. They cut a 29-point lead to four in the third, but the Warriors cruised to the win anyway thanks largely to shooting 46 percent from 3-point range, taking advantage of the Kings' terrible perimeter defense.

"Obviously, it's tough to win in this league with our team right now; we haven't figured that out at all," Murray said. "We can't put four quarters together to put ourselves in that position, so mindset-wise, we just got to take these couple days and just figure it out."

The Kings didn't have a counterpunch to the Warriors' decision to blitz point guard Darius Acuff Jr. with double teams. The talented rookie finished with 22 points but only had four in the first half when the game got away. He finished with another inefficient shooting night, making just nine of 24 shots.

Acuff has been just about the only bright spot from an otherwise terrible Kings season to date. But as much promise as he's shown, the 20-year-old is going to learn things the hard way, like how to manage opponents double-teaming him to force Sacramento's secondary and tertiary options to beat them.

"I think more than anything, you're going to need three outlets when you're going to blitz Darius," Kings coach Doug Christie said. "… I think we were a little late on everything in that first half, even on rotations. We have to be aggressive, and that was something that we went over in practice. It's something that we looked at in film and talked to them about."

The Kings rank dead last in offensive rating at 107.1 and dead last in defensive rating at 119.6.

They have a difficult game Sunday, when they host the 14-5 Minnesota Timberwolves before completing the home back-to-back against the 5-14 New Orleans Pelicans on Monday.

Wednesday continued what seems to be a years-long trend of the Kings struggling against teams who are missing their best players. And that was the case Monday and Wednesday by losing to the Spurs and Warriors with Wembanyama and Curry sidelined.

De'Anthony Melton torched the Kings by making eight of 12 from deep, leading to 24 points, while Gui Santos added 22 off the bench. Golden State won the battle of bench scoring, 61-19.

"San Antonio and Golden State both play good basketball with their guys moving the ball, getting good looks," Murray said. "Golden State shoots more threes without Steph, and it's more of just kick off and catch and shoot. And obviously, they have guys like Melton get hot. Teams like that have won in the past that are short-handed — just know how to win games."

#2721. Landeskog Calls Out Canadiens’ Late-Game Antics After Avalanche Blowout

Gabe Landeskog had no issue with the Montreal Canadiens wanting to defend their teammate. He just didn't like how far they went to do it.

"I've been on the other side of that. I've done kind of similar things that they've done," Landeskog said after the Avalanche's 6-0 win over the Canadiens on Wednesday at Ball Arena. "Try to get a response from somebody, and when it's not there, you can't really start cross-checking people, start slew-footing people."

Landeskog was referring to the final stages of a lopsided game that turned increasingly chippy after Kaiden Guhle left with an injury following a hit from the Avalanche captain. The Canadiens spent much of the remainder of the night looking for retribution.

"Well, listen, it was a battle. We had two battles all night," Landeskog said of his run-ins with Guhle. "First one he won. He knocked me on my ass. Almost got a high ankle sprain. And I got him back the second time. I wasn't trying to hurt him. I hope he's okay. He knew I was coming. Just kind of finished my check. So the whole time I saw that between me and him, hope he's okay, and that's that."

Kaiden Guhle left the game Wednesday night due to this hit from Gabriel Landeskog#NHL pic.twitter.com/cYzV4TxNFs

The Canadiens' two toughest players, the Xhekaj brothers, were the ones attempting to escalate things.

Florian Xhekaj tried to fight Landeskog late in the second period, but the Avalanche captain didn't take the bait. Instead, he drew a penalty, and Colorado scored on the ensuing power play.

Arber Xhekaj was involved in plenty of the late-game chaos, too. With four minutes remaining, he hit Brent Burns from behind and drew a crowd. Nazem Kadri and Martin Necas were the first players in.

Meanwhile, Florian was near the Avalanche bench and slashed Landeskog while he was sitting on the bench. Both brothers could face further scrutiny from the league before their next game.

"The Xhekaj brothers wanted to get involved, and we'll see what's going to happen with that," Landeskog said. "But I think for one of the most storied franchises and one of the most respected franchises in all of hockey, and then you got two guys doing that. I think at the end of the day, the way I see it, there's a battle between me and [Guhle] and then those guys want to try to get involved, and I get it, I do. But to a certain degree."

On the scoreboard, the Avalanche absolutely dominated Montreal. Mackenzie Blackwood made 27 saves to earn his second shutout, while Devon Toews scored twice. Logan O'Connor, Artturi Lehkonen, Necas, and Landeskog also found the back of the net.

Cale Makar added three assists in his return to the lineup. Brock Nelson and T.J. Hughes each had two assists, while Lehkonen and Necas also finished with multiple points.

It was one of Colorado's most complete performances of the season. Those types of efforts have become more common in recent weeks, even with superstar center Nathan MacKinnon out of the lineup.

But the Avalanche's response to Montreal's physicality might have been the most telling part of the night.

Following the late penalty on Arber for his hit on Burns, head coach Jared Bednar sent his top power-play unit over the boards. Colorado already had a 5-0 lead but wasn't interested in taking its foot off the gas. Landeskog, fittingly, scored the game's final goal. That decision was not an accident.

"I want to be respectful. I do. If it was a hooking penalty or tripping penalty, I would have given, like Parker Kelly and OC a crack and put different guys that don't play the power play," Bednar said. "But if part of their thing was we're gonna take some liberties on your guys, that's our response. If they're gonna take liberties on our guys, and guys are borderline getting hurt, then we're gonna put our unit out and keep doing some work."

#2719. Beware higher correlation but nab high U.S. credit yields, fund managers say

The artificial intelligence boom could lead to a recession, whether through a collapse in AI investment or widespread job losses as the technology advances, a hedge fund manager said.

"I do worry that if we don't have an AI-led recession in a few years, we're going to have an unemployment-led recession," Sheru Chowdhry, founder and chief investment officer at DSC Meridian Capital, said during a panel discussion moderated by CNBC's Joanna Ossinger at the iConnections Global Alts Asia conference in Singapore last week.

Chowdhry's warning highlights a risk that extends beyond a potential bursting of the AI bubble. Rapid advances in the technology could lead to widespread job losses, with implications for the housing market and consumer spending, he said.

"If this continues to go at the pace it is, I think in a few years a lot of us will probably not have jobs," Chowdhry said, adding that investors would have to rethink their approach to sectors such as housing and discretionary spending.

Even so, the fund manager remains bullish on computing power and sees opportunities in data centers, despite concerns about AI valuations.

The current AI investment cycle reminds Chowdhry of the dot-com bubble and the telecommunications infrastructure buildout of the early 2000s.

"This cycle reminds me of the dot-com bubble and the telecom build we had going on in the early 2000s," he said. "Every cycle is different, but it kind of rhymes in some ways."

Chowdhry also questioned whether semiconductor companies could sustain their profit margins in the coming years.

However, he sees opportunities in data centers, pointing to access to power and grid connections as major bottlenecks in the AI buildout.

"If you can identify data centers, I feel like that's going to be the picks and shovels of this cycle," he said.

In particular, data centers that can secure permits and access to power within the next two years present a significant investment opportunity, he added.

Beyond AI infrastructure, Chowdhry also pointed to attractive yields in U.S. credit markets.

He cited yields of around 8.5% on U.S. high-yield bonds and 9.5% on leveraged loans, describing the current market environment as an opportunity to "get paid to wait."

Investment portfolios may be more vulnerable to market volatility than they appear, according to Dan Gelernter, director of AI at R.G. Niederhoffer Capital Management, who spoke on the same panel.

Average monthly correlations across investable assets rose from around 5% between the 1960s and 1990s to roughly 11% to 12% by 2020, according to research cited by Gelernter.

His own analysis suggests that the figure has climbed further to around 15% to 16% in 2026.

"Most portfolios are more highly levered, less liquid, more highly correlated than they appear to be, and that problem will get worse the more markets move," Gelernter said.

Within the AI trade, Joo Kian Ong, a partner at AP3X Capital, said Samsung Electronics would be a good long position, with SK Hynix or Micron on the other side as potential shorts.
